Rapid uncovered boiling until dry is not braising and risks scorching or drying out the pan. Deep frying followed by a raw glaze skips the moist heat step that defines braising entirely. Steaming without liquid or seasoning does not develop the same flavor depth as a proper braise.
  198. The correct answer is A (Cut away peel and pith with a knife, then cut segments free from membranes): supreming citrus involves cutting off the peel and white pith with a sharp knife following the fruit's curve, then slicing each segment away from its surrounding membrane for a clean, pith free result. A vegetable peeler alone usually leaves pith behind and does not remove membranes. Boiling is unnecessary and would cook the fruit, changing its texture undesirably. Leaving skin attached defeats the purpose of a clean citrus supreme.
  199. The correct answer is A (Thin matchstick shaped strips of uniform width and length): julienne is defined by thin, uniform matchstick strips, typically around 3 millimeters by 3 millimeters by several centimeters long, which cook quickly and evenly in high heat applications like stir frying. Large rough chunks describe a rustic cut, not julienne. While a mandoline can produce thin slices, julienne specifically means strip shaped pieces, not round slices. Small cube shapes describe a brunoise or dice, a different cut entirely.
  200. The correct answer is B (Thoroughly dry the potatoes before frying to prevent oil spatter): excess surface water causes dangerous oil spatter and steam that prevents proper browning, so potatoes must be dried well before frying for both safety and a crisp finish. Adding wet potatoes directly to hot oil creates a serious burn hazard from violent spattering. Freezing wet potatoes is not standard procedure and would introduce ice crystals that worsen spattering. Pouring soaking water into a fryer is an extreme safety hazard that must never be done.
  201. The correct answer is C (Char the pepper skin under high heat, then steam in a covered container to loosen it): charring the skin under a broiler or open flame develops smoky flavor, and steaming the pepper afterward in a covered bowl loosens the blackened skin so it peels away easily. Raw pepper skin does not peel cleanly with a knife and wastes flesh in the process. Boiling would not dissolve the skin and instead waterlogs the flesh while losing smoky flavor entirely. Freezing then microwaving is not a recognized or safe method for this preparation.
  202. The correct answer is A (Stack julienne strips and crosscut them into tiny even cubes): brunoise is produced by first cutting the vegetable into fine julienne strips, then crosscutting those strips into tiny, uniform cube shapes suitable for a refined garnish. Random chopping without a julienne step produces uneven, irregular pieces unsuitable for the elegant brunoise standard. Grating changes the texture entirely and does not produce distinct cube shapes. Pureeing destroys the vegetable structure completely, making it impossible to reform into cubes.
  203. The correct answer is B (Steam or briefly blanch the broccoli, cooking only until crisp tender): steaming or a quick blanch uses minimal water contact and short cooking time, which preserves water soluble vitamins and keeps the bright green color and crisp texture intact. Prolonged boiling leaches nutrients into the discarded cooking water and dulls the color. A long, slow simmer overcooks the broccoli, degrading both texture and nutrient content. Deep frying at low temperature extends cooking time and adds fat without preserving nutrients or color.
  204. The correct answer is B (Bend each spear near the base until it naturally snaps at the tender point): asparagus naturally breaks at the point where the woody, fibrous section ends and the tender part begins, making this bend and snap method efficient and low waste. Cutting a fixed top half ignores natural variation in spear thickness and toughness. Peeling the entire spear before trimming wastes time and is unnecessary for most asparagus. Discarding the tender tips and keeping the tough base would produce an unusable, fibrous final product.
  205. The correct answer is A (Slice the leek lengthwise, then fan the layers under running water to flush out soil): leeks trap soil deep between their tightly wrapped layers as they grow, so splitting them lengthwise and fanning the layers open under running water is necessary to fully flush out grit. A quick rinse of the whole, uncut leek will not reach the soil trapped between inner layers. Soaking in oil does not address soil removal and is not a standard cleaning method. Wiping dry without water will not remove embedded dirt at all.
  206. The correct answer is B (Spread the vegetables in a single layer on the sheet pan with space between pieces): giving vegetables room on the pan allows hot dry air to circulate around each piece, promoting browning through the Maillard reaction rather than trapping steam. Overcrowding causes vegetables to steam in their own released moisture instead of roasting properly. Covering the pan tightly with foil traps steam for the entire cook, preventing browning. Adding extra liquid moves the technique away from roasting and toward braising or steaming.
  207. The correct answer is C (Score the tomato, blanch briefly, shock in ice water, peel, then seed and dice): scoring the skin and briefly blanching loosens it so it slips off easily after an ice water shock, after which the tomato is halved to remove seeds and diced for the finished concasse. Peeling raw tomato skin with a knife wastes flesh and is far slower than the blanch method. Freezing and grating destroys the tomato's structure and is not standard practice. Deep frying is not used for this preparation and would cook the tomato rather than simply loosening the skin.
  208. The correct answer is C (Submerge the potatoes in cold water and refrigerate until needed): keeping cut potatoes submerged in cold water limits oxygen exposure to prevent browning, and refrigeration keeps them out of the temperature danger zone to limit bacterial growth. Leaving them exposed to air at room temperature causes both browning and unsafe temperature holding. Wrapping in foil without refrigeration does not address the temperature danger zone risk. Direct sunlight would raise the temperature further and promote both browning and bacterial growth.
  209. The correct answer is A (Stack, roll tightly, then slice thinly across the roll with a sharp knife): chiffonade is made by stacking clean, dry basil leaves, rolling them tightly, and slicing thin ribbons across the roll with a sharp blade, which minimizes bruising and produces a clean garnish. Tearing by hand produces uneven, rough pieces rather than uniform ribbons. Repeated chopping with a dull blade bruises the leaves and causes them to blacken quickly. Crushing the leaves first releases oils and darkens them before the cut is even made.
  210. The correct answer is C (Large volume helps the water return to a boil quickly after vegetables are added): a large volume of water loses less overall heat when cold vegetables are added, so it returns to a rolling boil quickly, keeping cooking time short and preserving color and texture. A slower return to boil, not a slower overall boil, would actually risk overcooking and dulling color. The salt concentration is a separate seasoning decision unrelated to water volume for temperature recovery. Large volume blanching benefits leafy greens and delicate vegetables just as much as root vegetables.
  211. The correct answer is A (Cook the onions slowly over low to medium heat, stirring occasionally over an extended time): true caramelization requires slow, gentle heat over a long period to break down the onion's natural sugars gradually into deep golden brown color and rich sweetness. High heat for only five minutes will brown the surface unevenly without developing full caramelized flavor. Boiling in water cooks the onions through steam and moisture rather than dry heat browning, so no caramelization occurs. Deep frying at very high heat browns too quickly and risks burning before sweetness develops.
  212. The correct answer is D (Store the avocados at room temperature, optionally near a ripe banana to boost ethylene exposure): room temperature storage allows natural ripening to continue, and placing avocados near a high ethylene producer like a ripe banana speeds the process for next day use. Refrigeration slows ripening, which works against the goal of having ripe avocados by the next day. Freezing whole avocados does not ripen them and instead destroys their texture through ice crystal damage. Ice packs would chill the fruit and slow ripening rather than accelerate it.
  213. The correct answer is C (Add denser, slower cooking vegetables first, followed by quicker cooking items later): staggering the addition of vegetables based on density and cooking time, starting with carrots and adding delicate items like bean sprouts near the end, ensures every component reaches proper doneness at the same finishing point. Adding everything simultaneously would leave dense vegetables undercooked while delicate ones turn mushy. Reversing the order and adding fast cooking items first would badly overcook them before the dense vegetables are ready. Cooking each item in separate uncoordinated pans loses the point of a unified stir fry and complicates timing unnecessarily.
  214. The correct answer is B (Add an acid such as vinegar early and keep the slaw well chilled until service): acid helps stabilize the anthocyanin pigments in red cabbage, reducing excessive color bleeding, and chilling limits enzymatic activity and moisture release that would otherwise stain other ingredients. Cooking the cabbage would eliminate the crisp raw texture expected in a slaw. Soaking in warm water promotes both color bleeding and softening, working against the desired crisp result. Chopping as finely as possible for maximum bleed directly contradicts the goal of limiting color transfer.
  215. The correct answer is D (Foil wrapping traps steam, which helps tenderize the dense beet flesh evenly): wrapping beets in foil creates a moist, steamy micro environment inside the packet that helps the dense root cook through evenly without drying out. It does not prevent cooking, since heat still penetrates the foil and beets fully cook this way. Foil has no effect on the beet's natural color, which comes from its pigments. While roasting softens the skin, peeling is still typically required afterward, so wrapping does not eliminate that step.
  216. The correct answer is D (Peel using a sharp chef knife to cut away the thick, uneven skin in sections): celeriac has a rough, knobby, thick skin that a standard peeler cannot navigate effectively, so a sharp chef knife cutting in sections around the contours is the safer and more efficient method. A standard vegetable peeler struggles with the uneven surface and often leaves patches of skin behind. The tough outer skin does not soften enough during cooking to be palatable and should be removed. Torching is not a standard or safe method for peeling this vegetable.
  217. The correct answer is C (Cook the spinach briefly over high heat and drain any released liquid immediately): spinach wilts and releases significant water almost instantly, so a quick high heat saute followed by draining excess liquid keeps the dish from becoming waterlogged. A long simmer in a large volume of water is essentially boiling, which leaches nutrients and overcooks the delicate leaves. Covering the pan for an extended time traps steam and worsens the wateriness problem. Adding fat before any heat is applied does not address the moisture issue and delays proper cooking.
  218. The correct answer is C (Cook the corn briefly, just until the kernels are tender, avoiding prolonged cooking): fresh corn kernels cook quickly, and prolonged boiling toughens the kernels and dulls natural sweetness, so a short cook time preserves the best eating quality. Boiling for thirty minutes far exceeds what is needed and produces a tough, less flavorful result. Adding sugar to the water is unnecessary for fresh, in season corn with adequate natural sweetness. Frozen corn requires different timing than fresh corn since it is often partially cooked before freezing, so treating them identically risks overcooking.
  219. The correct answer is A (Salt sliced eggplant and let it rest to draw out excess moisture and bitterness before rinsing and grilling): salting draws out bitter compounds and excess moisture through osmosis, and rinsing afterward removes the surface salt before the eggplant goes onto the grill. Soaking in sugar water is not a standard technique for reducing bitterness in eggplant. Skipping the salting step can leave a more bitter, watery result, especially in older eggplants. Freezing before slicing changes the cell structure and texture in an undesirable way and does not address bitterness.
  220. The correct answer is B (Blanch briefly in boiling salted water, then shock immediately in ice water): fresh peas are tender and cook quickly, so a brief blanch followed by an ice water shock preserves their bright green color and sweet flavor for a smooth, vibrant puree. Slow braising for over an hour is far too long for a delicate vegetable like peas and would dull both color and flavor. Deep frying changes the texture entirely and is not standard for a puree preparation. Extended steaming until fully broken down overcooks the peas well past the point of ideal flavor and color retention.
  221. The correct answer is B (Crush cloves lightly with the flat of a knife to loosen skins, or shake cloves vigorously in a covered container): lightly crushing separated cloves loosens the papery skin so it slips off easily, and vigorously shaking cloves in a covered container also separates skins mechanically for efficient large batch peeling. Peeling each clove individually by hand with a knife is far slower for a large volume task. Boiling whole heads for two hours is excessive and better suited to a different preparation, not efficient peeling. Microwaving until charred damages the garlic and is not a controlled or standard peeling method.
  222. The correct answer is A (Use a sharp knife with a rocking motion, allowing the blade to cut cleanly through the leaves): a sharp blade cuts cleanly through herb cells rather than crushing them, and a rocking motion maintains control and even chopping without bruising the delicate leaves. A dull knife tears cell walls and releases excess moisture and dark bruising. Crushing the leaves before chopping damages them and darkens the color prematurely. Chopping wet herbs causes clumping and a soggy, bruised texture rather than a clean, dry chop.
  223. The correct answer is B (Use a sturdy chef knife to trim away the tough skin in controlled strokes on a stable cutting board): butternut squash skin is often too thick and hard for a standard peeler, so using a sturdy chef knife with controlled strokes on a stable, secured cutting board is the safer and more effective approach. Forcing a peeler against tough resistance increases the risk of slipping and injury. Microwaving briefly can help soften skin in some cases but is not the primary safe cutting method and does not replace proper knife technique. Peeling or cutting while holding the squash unsupported in one hand significantly increases the risk of a knife injury.
  224. The correct answer is C (Hold the stem at the top and strip leaves downward against the direction of growth): Herb growth runs from the base of the stem toward the tip, so holding the stem at the top and stripping down toward the base moves against that growth direction and pulls leaves free cleanly in one motion with minimal bruising. This is efficient for high volume prep. Removing leaves individually by hand is far too slow for a large batch during busy prep. Chopping stems together with leaves and sorting afterward wastes time and risks bitter stem pieces ending up in the finished garnish.
  225. The correct answer is A (The broccoli was left in boiling water too long before shocking): overcooking before shocking causes chlorophyll to break down into dull olive pigments and softens the cell structure past the point ice water can rescue, producing both dull color and mushy texture together. Under salting affects seasoning, not color or texture breakdown of this kind. The ice water itself cannot be too cold for this purpose, since the goal is to stop cooking as fast as possible. Overly small pieces would cook even faster, making overcooking more likely, but the root problem described is excessive blanch time, not piece size alone.
  226. The correct answer is B (Trapped moisture in a sealed container with no air circulation, promoting mold growth on delicate berries): sealing delicate, high moisture berries without ventilation traps condensation against the fruit's skin, creating ideal conditions for mold growth and rapid textural breakdown. Refrigeration itself is appropriate and beneficial for strawberries, so cold temperature is not the cause of this failure. Strawberries are commonly and successfully refrigerated in professional kitchens when handled correctly. The described failure involves trapped moisture, not excess airflow or drying out, which would produce shriveling rather than mold and mushiness.
  227. The correct answer is D (The vegetables were cut into significantly different sizes before roasting): when pieces vary widely in size, smaller pieces caramelize and finish well before larger pieces reach doneness, producing the exact pattern of some cooked pieces alongside pale, undercooked ones in the same batch. Briefly opening the oven door causes minor temperature fluctuation but would not create this dramatic piece by piece inconsistency. A dark pan can promote browning overall but affects the whole tray somewhat evenly, not selectively by piece. Salt quantity affects flavor, not the physical browning and doneness of individual pieces.
  228. The correct answer is D (Bananas and apples have enzymes that oxidize when their cut flesh is exposed to air, while whole grapes were not cut): enzymatic browning occurs specifically at cut surfaces exposed to oxygen, and since apples and bananas were sliced while grapes typically remain whole or are only halved with less exposed surface, the browning appears selective. Sugar content is not the determining factor in enzymatic browning. All the fruit in a single salad container would experience the same storage temperature, so that cannot explain the selective effect. Light exposure is not the primary driver of enzymatic browning in cut fruit; oxygen exposure is.
  229. The correct answer is C (Ethylene gas released by the ripening pears triggered a bitter compound response in the carrots): carrots are known to develop increased bitterness when exposed to ethylene gas from nearby ripening fruit like pears, since the gas triggers a stress compound response in the root vegetable. There is no salt source described in this scenario that would explain the bitterness. The scenario does not indicate an inappropriate cooler temperature for pears, and pear ripening at cooler temperatures is normal. While age can affect quality, the specific detail about proximity to ripening pears points directly to ethylene exposure as the more precise explanation.
  230. The correct answer is A (The mushrooms were overcrowded in the pan, causing them to steam instead of sear): overcrowding lowers the pan temperature and traps the moisture mushrooms naturally release, causing them to steam and turn rubbery rather than developing a browned, seared exterior. Too little oil would more likely cause sticking or burning rather than excess liquid release. Preheating the pan properly is actually a correct step for good searing, not a cause of this problem. While early salting can draw out some moisture, the primary cause of significant liquid pooling and rubbery texture in this scenario is overcrowding, which prevents evaporation.
  231. The correct answer is C (Insufficient liquid was used, or the pot lid did not seal well enough to retain moisture during the long cook): braising relies on enough liquid and a well sealed cover to sustain moist heat over an extended time, so scorching and a nearly dry pot point to either starting with too little liquid or losing too much through evaporation from a poor seal. Too much liquid would leave excess sauce rather than a scorched, dry pot. Cabbage piece size affects cooking time and tenderness but would not by itself cause the liquid to disappear and the pot to scorch. Cooking at too low a temperature throughout would more likely leave the dish undercooked rather than scorched and dry.
  232. The correct answer is D (Enzymatic browning from air exposure combined with a food safety risk from extended time in the temperature danger zone): uncovered, unrefrigerated cut potatoes will oxidize and brown from air exposure, and two hours at room temperature also pushes the product toward or into the temperature danger zone where bacterial growth accelerates, which can explain the off smell. This is not purely aesthetic, since the off smell signals a potential safety issue beyond appearance. Browning and off odors do not indicate improved flavor, they indicate quality and safety deterioration. The scenario explicitly describes both a visual change and a smell change, so it is not limited to appearance alone.
  233. The correct answer is B (Prolonged exposure to heat during extended holding degraded the chlorophyll further): even properly blanched and shocked vegetables will gradually lose their bright color when held at hot holding temperatures for an extended service period, since continued heat exposure breaks down chlorophyll over time. Undercooking during the initial blanch would more likely leave the beans too firm rather than explain a color change that occurs specifically during later holding. A steam table set too cold would raise food safety concerns about temperature, not explain a color shift caused by heat exposure. While skipping the ice water shock could contribute to early color loss, the scenario specifically describes color change happening during reheating and holding, pointing to prolonged heat exposure as the driving factor.
  234. The correct answer is B (Accelerate ripening using controlled ethylene exposure and warmer temporary storage, or substitute a ready to use item if time does not allow): exposing green bananas to a concentrated ethylene source and holding them at a warmer temperature can speed ripening somewhat, but if same day service is truly required, the more reliable fix is substituting a ready to use item to avoid serving underripe fruit. Serving green bananas as is would produce starchy, unpleasant texture and flavor unsuitable for a dessert application. Refrigeration slows ripening rather than speeding it, working directly against the goal. Freezing whole bananas does not ripen them and instead destroys their texture, making this option unusable for the dessert.
  235. The correct answer is A (The wok was overcrowded and the vegetables were cooked too long, releasing moisture that steamed rather than seared them): overcrowding drops pan temperature and traps released moisture, and extended cooking time compounds the problem, together producing the limp, gray, liquid pooled result described rather than a properly seared, vibrant stir fry. Oil quality is not a factor that would cause this specific combination of limpness and liquid pooling. Uniform cutting is actually a correct technique that supports even cooking, not a fault. A properly preheated wok is a correct step for good searing, not a cause of this failure, so excessive preheat time alone does not explain the described outcome as well as overcrowding and overcooking together.
  236. The correct answer is B (The avocado flesh was exposed to air, and pressing plastic wrap directly onto the surface along with added acid would slow the oxidation): exposed avocado flesh oxidizes quickly, and minimizing air contact with direct plastic wrap contact plus adding acid such as lime juice both slow the enzymatic browning reaction significantly. Refrigeration does slow the reaction rate somewhat by lowering enzyme activity, so browning is not entirely unavoidable or unaffected by temperature. Storing the guacamole uncovered would increase air exposure and worsen browning, the opposite of a helpful fix. Salt affects flavor and can have a minor drawing effect but does not function as a primary browning prevention method compared to reducing air exposure and adding acid.
  237. The correct answer is D (The vegetables were overcrowded on the pan and were still wet from washing when roasted): excess surface moisture from incomplete drying combined with overcrowding traps steam around the vegetables, preventing the dry heat browning needed for caramelization and resulting in a soggy texture. Tossing with oil right before roasting is standard, correct practice and does not cause sogginess. A centered oven rack is a normal, appropriate position for even roasting and is not a fault. Cutting vegetables into small, uniform pieces actually supports even cooking and is not itself a cause of sogginess unless combined with overcrowding or excess moisture.
  238. The correct answer is D (Trapped moisture against the leaves with no airflow accelerated bacterial growth and decay): wrapping wet herbs tightly in plastic with no ventilation traps moisture directly against the leaf surface, creating conditions that promote rapid bacterial growth, sliminess, and blackened decay. The refrigerator temperature is not described as inadequate, and proper refrigeration paired with poor moisture management is still enough to cause this failure. Cilantro does not naturally blacken this quickly under proper storage conditions, so storage method is clearly the driving factor. Standard food safe plastic film does not chemically react with cilantro leaves to cause this kind of decay.
  239. The correct answer is C (Oil applied before roasting improves heat conduction and helps drive the Maillard browning reaction on the surface): coating vegetables in oil before roasting improves contact with the hot pan surface and promotes the Maillard reaction responsible for deep browning and complex flavor development. Salt applied after cooking sits on the surface rather than penetrating deeply, since deep penetration generally requires time and often occurs before cooking. The scenario's outcome directly shows that seasoning order does affect browning, contradicting the claim of no measurable effect. Oil plays a clear functional role in browning through improved heat transfer, not just a flavor role.
  240. The correct answer is D (Choose the asparagus since it is at peak local season, offering better flavor, quality, and typically lower cost): featuring asparagus during its local spring peak season takes advantage of superior flavor, better quality, and generally lower cost compared to an out of season imported item that has traveled long distances and lost some quality along the way. Seasonality significantly affects flavor and cost, so treating any vegetable as equally suitable regardless of season ignores an important purchasing factor. Shelf life in storage is a secondary consideration and does not outweigh the flavor and cost advantages of peak season local produce. Appearance alone is an incomplete basis for menu decisions when flavor, quality, and cost all favor the seasonal choice.
  241. The correct answer is C (Stock is often reduced later, so salt would concentrate to an unusable level): Stock is a building block for sauces, soups, and braises that are frequently reduced, and reduction concentrates any salt already present. Leaving stock unseasoned lets the cook control final seasoning in the finished dish. Salt has no meaningful effect on gelatin formation or storage life. This principle applies to every stock type covered in the chapter.
  242. The correct answer is D (Brown stock bones and mirepoix are roasted or caramelized before simmering): Roasting the bones and caramelizing the mirepoix creates Maillard browning, which produces the dark colour and deep roasted flavour that defines brown stock. White stock uses bones that are not browned at all. Bone type is not what separates the two categories. Both stock types typically include a sachet or bouquet garni.
  243. The correct answer is B (Cooking removes the raw acidic taste and builds deeper flavour and colour): Cooking the tomato paste with the mirepoix caramelizes its sugars and removes the harsh raw acidity, contributing to the depth of colour and flavour expected in brown stock. Tomato paste contains no gelatin. This step happens before liquid is added, not after boiling begins. Cloudiness is controlled through simmer temperature, not tomato paste handling.
  244. The correct answer is C (Salmon, because its oily, strong flavour overwhelms the delicate stock): Oily fish such as salmon and mackerel are avoided for classical fumet because their strong flavour and fat content dominate the finished stock. Lean white fish like sole, halibut, and turbot are preferred for their delicate, clean flavour. These lean fish bones cook quickly and release gelatin efficiently. None of the lean fish options are the correct answer here.
  245. The correct answer is B (20 to 30 minutes, since fish bones release flavour quickly): Fish bones are soft and delicate compared to beef or veal bones, so they release their flavour and gelatin quickly. A short simmer of 20 to 30 minutes is standard, and cooking much longer than this makes the stock bitter and cloudy. Long simmer times of several hours apply to heavier meat stocks, not fish stock. This short cooking window is a frequently tested detail.
  246. The correct answer is C (Potatoes, beets, and cabbage family vegetables): Starchy vegetables like potatoes cloud the liquid, beets discolour it, and cabbage family vegetables can create strong, dominating flavours. Leek, celery root, and mushroom trim are commonly added to broaden vegetable stock flavour. Standard mirepoix vegetables form the stock's base rather than being avoided. Herbs and aromatics are used for flavouring, not excluded for clouding reasons.
  247. The correct answer is A (A second, weaker stock made by resimmering the same bones a second time): Remouillage means rewetting, and it refers to simmering already-used bones a second time to extract remaining flavour and gelatin. It produces a weaker liquid than the original stock and is typically used as cooking liquid for the next batch instead of plain water. It is unrelated to cream finishing, skimming, or clarification with egg whites. Using remouillage reduces food cost by making full use of the bones.
  248. The correct answer is C (Collagen in connective tissue, cartilage, and bones converting during cooking): Collagen found in connective tissue, cartilage, and bone converts to gelatin during long, slow simmering, giving properly made stock a light set when chilled. Vegetable starch and rendered fat do not provide gelatin. Egg whites are used for clarification, not gelatin production. Knuckle bones and feet are prized specifically because they are rich in collagen.
  249. The correct answer is C (Two parts onion to one part carrot to one part celery): The classical mirepoix ratio is two parts onion to one part carrot to one part celery by weight. This ratio is a common calculation question on the exam, where a total weight is given and candidates must divide it correctly. Equal thirds, or ratios favouring carrot or celery, do not match the classical standard. White mirepoix substitutes leek and parsnip but keeps a similar proportional logic.
  250. The correct answer is B (Leeks and parsnips, sometimes with mushroom trim added): White mirepoix substitutes leeks and parsnips for carrot, and mushroom trim is sometimes included, keeping the stock pale for white sauces and light coloured soups. Beets would actually discolour the stock rather than keep it pale. Bell peppers, zucchini, sweet potato, and squash are not classical white mirepoix components. This substitution preserves colour while still building aromatic flavour.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (A sachet is wrapped in cheesecloth while a bouquet garni is tied with twine): A sachet d'epices bundles peppercorns, bay leaf, thyme, and parsley stems in cheesecloth, while a bouquet garni ties similar aromatics together with kitchen twine, often using celery or leek greens as the wrapper. Garlic inclusion is not the defining difference between them. Neither is restricted to a single stock type. Both are added early and simmered throughout, not added only at the end.
  2. The correct answer is D (Sauteing would add unwanted colour and fat to a stock meant to stay pale): The long simmer time in stock production is enough to extract flavour from raw aromatics, and sauteing would introduce colour and fat that conflict with the pale, clean appearance expected of white stock. Vegetables do not contain gelatin. Cloudiness relates to simmer temperature and skimming, not raw versus sauteed vegetables. Vegetables can withstand the simmer time without needing to be precooked.
  3. The correct answer is C (Cold water allows impurities to be drawn out slowly and skimmed cleanly): Starting with cold water lets proteins and impurities release gradually as the liquid heats, so they can be skimmed off the surface for a clearer finished stock. Boiling water shocks the proteins, causing them to seize and trap impurities inside rather than releasing them. Cold water does not shorten cooking time or affect bone floating in a meaningful way. This is a foundational clarity principle tested throughout the chapter.
  4. The correct answer is B (To coagulate and trap impurities, clarifying the stock as it rises): The raft is made from ground meat, egg whites, mirepoix, and acid, and as its proteins coagulate they trap cloudy particles and impurities, rising to the surface to form a solid mat. Consomme remains unthickened, so the raft does not add body. It does not add fat since the raft is skimmed away, and it does not speed up cooking time. This clarification process defines what makes a consomme different from a regular stock.
  5. The correct answer is C (To let the consomme bubble up gently for ladling without disturbing the raft): The hole lets the consomme simmer and rise through a controlled opening, allowing the cook to ladle finished liquid out without breaking or disturbing the raft above it. It is not used for adding seasoning, releasing steam pressure, or draining fat specifically. Disturbing the raft at any point risks reintroducing cloudiness. This careful handling is essential to producing a perfectly clear finished product.
  6. The correct answer is D (It helps coagulation and slightly brightens the finished flavour and colour): Acid supports the coagulation process in the raft and contributes a slight brightness to the final flavour and colour of the consomme. It does not prevent browning, since the raft is not meant to brown at all. Consomme is unthickened, so acid plays no thickening role. Acid supplements rather than replaces the egg whites needed for clarification.
  7. The correct answer is B (Perfect clarity with a full, concentrated flavour): A properly finished consomme should be perfectly clear, amber to deep brown depending on the base stock, with a rich, concentrated flavour. It should never be thick or opaque, since it remains an unthickened clear soup. It is not meant to be pale or milky. The raft is fully strained out, so no visible chunks should remain in the finished product.
  8. The correct answer is C (Broth and bouillon, since both remain unthickened liquids): Clear soups include broth, bouillon, and consomme, all of which stay unthickened so that ingredients suspended in them remain visible. Bisque and chowder both rely on added thickening and belong to the thick soup family. Cream soup and puree soup are also thick soups, not clear soups. Veloute is a sauce base, not itself a clear soup category.
  9. The correct answer is D (Broth includes meat and is often seasoned, while stock stays unseasoned): Broth is typically made using meat as well as bones, is often seasoned, and can be served directly as a finished dish. Stock remains unseasoned and functions as a building block for other preparations rather than being served on its own. Neither broth nor stock is inherently thickened, since both fall under liquid bases. Vegetable stock is one type of stock, but stock is not defined by always containing bones exclusively.
  10. The correct answer is A (It relies on blended cooked vegetables or legumes for body, without dairy or roux): A puree soup gets its body from blending cooked starchy vegetables or legumes smooth, without needing added dairy or a roux. A liaison finish describes certain cream based soups, not puree soups specifically. Roasted shellfish shells describe bisque, not puree soup. Puree soups do not rely on a classical roux for their thickness.
  11. The correct answer is D (A veloute, meaning stock thickened with a light roux): Cream soups classically begin from a veloute base, which is stock lightly thickened with roux, then finished with cream or milk near the end of cooking. A raw unblended puree would not provide the smooth, cooked base expected. Consomme is a clear soup and is not thickened into a cream soup base. Demi-glace is far too rich and reduced to serve as a cream soup starting point.
  12. The correct answer is C (Roasted and simmered shellfish shells, most often lobster, shrimp, or crab): Bisque gets its deep, pronounced shellfish flavour from roasting and simmering the shells themselves, not just using the picked meat. Cream is added later to enrich and finish the soup, not to provide its core flavour. Vegetable trim and brown roux are not the defining flavour source for bisque. A clarifying raft belongs to consomme production, not bisque.
  13. The correct answer is A (Chowder contains distinct, visible chunks, while puree soup is blended smooth): Chowder is a hearty, chunky soup where solid ingredients like fish, shellfish, or vegetables remain in distinct pieces, unlike puree soup which is blended until smooth. Chowder is typically served hot, not cold. Chowder is commonly thickened with a roux, and often contains diced potato as a defining ingredient. These texture and ingredient differences are frequently tested distinctions.
  14. The correct answer is D (Minestrone, a chunky Italian vegetable soup often containing pasta or rice): Minestrone is the classical chunky Italian vegetable soup that often includes pasta or rice among its vegetables and legumes. Cock-a-leekie is a Scottish chicken and leek soup, not Italian. French onion soup features caramelized onions and a cheese topped crouton over beef stock. Bisque is a French shellfish based soup, unrelated to Italian vegetable preparations.
  15. The correct answer is A (A cheese topped crouton placed over caramelized onions and beef stock): French onion soup is classically finished with a crouton topped with melted cheese, served over caramelized onions in a rich beef stock base. A liaison finish belongs to certain cream soups, not French onion soup. Raw egg is not part of this classical preparation. Sour cream and shellfish shells describe entirely different soup styles.
  16. The correct answer is B (Thickening power decreases because prolonged cooking breaks down starch structure): As a roux darkens from white to brown, prolonged cooking breaks down some of the flour's starch structure, meaning a brown roux thickens less per unit weight than a white roux. Thickening power does not increase or stay constant with extended cooking time. The type of fat used does not change this starch breakdown relationship. This trade-off is why brown roux is valued more for flavour than for thickening strength.
  17. The correct answer is C (A raw starch dissolved in cold liquid, whisked in for a quick thickening fix): A slurry combines a raw starch like cornstarch or arrowroot with cold liquid, then gets whisked into a hot soup for fast, last minute thickening. A cooked fat and flour mixture describes roux, not slurry. Egg yolks and cream describe a liaison. Softened butter kneaded with raw flour describes beurre manie, a different thickening method entirely.
  18. The correct answer is C (Tempering prevents the raw egg yolks from scrambling in the hot liquid): Tempering gradually raises the temperature of the egg yolk and cream mixture by whisking in small amounts of hot liquid first, preventing the yolks from scrambling when added to the pot. It does not remove fat from the cream. Tempering does not thicken the liaison itself, since thickening happens once it is incorporated and gently warmed. Arrowroot and cornstarch relate to slurries, not liaisons.
  19. The correct answer is B (A raw butter and flour paste used for small, last minute adjustments to a finished soup): Beurre manie is a raw, uncooked mixture of equal parts softened butter and flour, whisked into a simmering liquid in small pieces for quick corrections. It is not used to thicken a large batch from the beginning, since raw flour needs a bit of simmering afterward to lose its starchy taste. It has no connection to sauteing fish or clarified butter. It differs from a liaison, which uses egg yolk and cream rather than butter and flour.
  20. The correct answer is D (The natural starch in cooked vegetables or legumes provides body once blended smooth): When starchy vegetables or legumes are cooked until soft and blended smooth, their natural starch provides thickening body without any added fat or flour. Gelatin from bones is unrelated to vegetable puree thickening. Egg white rafts are used for clarifying consomme, not thickening puree soups. Puree soups do not require a roux, since the puree itself supplies the body.
  21. The correct answer is B (Knuckle bones and feet, which are rich in collagen): Knuckle bones and feet contain high amounts of connective tissue and cartilage, both rich in collagen, which converts to gelatin during a long simmer and produces strong body in the finished stock. Lean loin trim has comparatively little connective tissue to offer. Fully trimmed rib bones lose much of the collagen that would otherwise help. Animal age alone does not determine collagen content the way bone type and cut do.

Stirring does not meaningfully affect cooling speed or clarification chemistry directly. It has nothing to do with the sachet releasing bitterness. Evaporation rate is not the primary concern behind avoiding stirring at this stage.
  23. The correct answer is D (3 kilograms of onion, following the standard two-part ratio): In a two to one to one ratio, the total mirepoix divides into four equal parts, with onion taking two of those parts. Six kilograms divided into four parts equals 1.5 kilograms per part, so onion at two parts equals 3 kilograms. Equal thirds or a 4:1:1 split do not match the classical ratio. Carrot and celery would each receive 1.5 kilograms in this correct breakdown.
  24. The correct answer is A (The stock was allowed to boil instead of maintaining a gentle simmer): Boiling agitates fat and impurities into the liquid instead of letting them rise cleanly to the surface for skimming, which is the most common cause of cloudy stock.   25. It does not become richer or automatically thicken from extended cooking. It will not taste the same as a correctly timed batch, since the extra time actively damages the result. This timing sensitivity is unique to fish stock compared to heartier meat stocks.
  26. Freezing spent bones to reuse directly in a fresh batch is not standard remouillage procedure.

Pouring cold liaison directly into boiling soup would scramble the eggs immediately. Adding it at the very start defeats the purpose of a liaison, which finishes a soup near the end. Boiling after adding a liaison curdles the eggs and ruins the texture.
  29. Extending the simmer by an hour would over-reduce and overcook the sauce rather than provide a quick fix.

This procedural fix reinforces why stock is never salted heavily during initial production.

This means the manager should not expect stronger thickening from switching to brown roux at equal weight. Thickening power is not identical between the two, since darkening directly changes the starch's ability to thicken. Switching roux colour does not eliminate the need for roux, since some thickening agent is still required to build the soup's body.

It is not restricted to cream based soups and can be used more broadly for adjustments. It does not require hours of refrigeration before use, since it is typically kneaded and used relatively quickly.   71. The correct answer is C (Whole milk thickened with a pale white roux cooked only briefly): bechamel is defined by milk as its liquid and a pale, briefly cooked white roux as its thickener. This short cooking time keeps the roux from developing color or a toasted flavor. Veloute uses stock and a blond roux, while espagnole uses brown stock and a brown roux, so mixing these up is a common exam trap.
  72. This tradeoff between flavor and thickening strength is tested often on the exam.
  74. This sets it apart from bechamel, veloute, espagnole, and tomato sauce, which all rely on roux or reduction for body.

  78. This is a classic and frequently tested exam trap.
  79. The correct answer is C (Cornstarch mixed with cold liquid before being whisked into a hot sauce): cornstarch is dissolved in cold liquid to form a slurry, then whisked into a hot sauce, producing a clear, glossy result unlike the slightly opaque look of a roux based sauce. This makes it well suited to fruit sauces and lighter modern preparations that need a clear appearance.
  80. The correct answer is D (Beurre manie is never precooked at all, while roux is cooked before liquid is added): beurre manie is a raw paste of equal parts softened butter and flour, whisked directly into a simmering sauce in small pieces as a fast finishing thickener. Roux, by contrast, is cooked on its own first to remove the raw flour taste before liquid is introduced.
  81. The correct answer is A (To gradually raise the egg yolk temperature so it does not instantly scramble on contact): tempering involves slowly whisking a small amount of hot liquid into the egg yolk mixture so its temperature rises gradually, before the warmed yolks are stirred back into the rest of the hot sauce, preventing curdling on contact with sudden heat.
  82. The correct answer is D (A temporary emulsion separates again after mixing, while a permanent one is held stable by an emulsifier): a basic vinaigrette is a temporary emulsion because it has no emulsifying agent and must be reshaken before use. A permanent emulsion, such as mayonnaise or hollandaise, contains an emulsifier like egg yolk that keeps fat droplets suspended over time.
  83. The correct answer is D (Lecithin, a compound that binds effectively to both fat and water): lecithin is a compound in egg yolk that binds to both fat and water, allowing it to coat tiny droplets of fat and hold them suspended within a water based liquid. Gelatin and collagen come from connective tissue in stocks, not emulsified sauces.
  84.   85. The correct answer is B (The fat was added far too quickly for the emulsifier to properly coat every droplet): emulsions typically break when fat is added faster than the emulsifier can coat the droplets, when the sauce gets too hot, or when it gets too cold and the fat solidifies again inside the pot.
  86.   87.   89.   90.   162. Lower-fat creams like half and half or table cream lack sufficient fat to whip properly. Evaporated milk is a concentrated, unwhippable product used for different culinary purposes.
  163. The correct answer is D (About 16 percent, the typical water content in standard commercial butter.): commercial butter is regulated to contain roughly 80 percent milk fat, about 16 to 18 percent water, and the remainder milk solids. This ratio is important when clarifying butter, since the water and solids are removed to leave pure butterfat. Knowing the water content also explains why butter spatters when heated.
  164. The correct answer is D (Fresh unripened cheese, consumed shortly after production with no aging period.): fresh unripened cheeses are consumed shortly after production, retain high moisture, and are not aged to develop rind or complex flavor. Fresh mozzarella and ricotta fall into this category because they are made and sold within days. Hard, semi-soft, and blue-veined cheeses all undergo a defined aging or culturing process that fresh cheese skips.
  165. The correct answer is A (Lactobacillus bulgaricus and Streptococcus thermophilus, the traditional yogurt starter culture pairing.): these two bacterial strains work symbiotically, converting lactose to lactic acid, which lowers the pH and denatures milk proteins so the mixture sets and thickens. This process also produces yogurt's tangy flavor and probiotic benefits. The other organisms listed are used for bread yeast, blue cheese mold, or other fermented foods, not standard yogurt.
  166. The correct answer is D (Low-fat milk fermented with lactic acid producing bacterial cultures.): commercial cultured buttermilk is made by adding specific bacterial cultures to pasteurized low-fat or skim milk, which thickens it and gives it a tangy flavor similar to yogurt. Traditional buttermilk, the liquid left after churning butter, is rarely sold commercially today. A quick acidulated milk of milk plus lemon juice is a substitute but not true cultured buttermilk.
  167. Evaporated milk remains unsweetened and is typically diluted with water to approximate fresh milk. This difference determines which product is appropriate for savory versus dessert applications.
  168. The correct answer is A (It is heated briefly to a much higher temperature than pasteurization.): UHT processing heats milk to around 138 degrees Celsius for a few seconds, destroying nearly all microorganisms and spores, then packages it aseptically. This combination allows sealed cartons to sit at room temperature for months. Once opened, UHT milk still requires refrigeration like any other dairy product.
  169.
